Transformer

0.7921\pm 0.0070

0.1222\pm 0.0003

Mamba2

0.0500\pm 0.0000

0.0021\pm 0.0000

Mamba-2 did not converge on SymbolSoup or Diffuse MQAR.
We view this as qualitatively consistent with our selective-SSM theory: when noise makes the selection signal weakly separable, the resulting non-vanishing freeze-time errors restore exponential attenuation of long-range influence, as formalized in Proposition
5
and Corollary
4.6
.
This interpretation is relevant to Mamba-2 because it is itself a selective SSM, specifically a scalar-identity restricted variant in the SSD framework
(
17
)
.

5.2
SimpleStories language modeling

5.2.1
Dataset and task

For the short-context regime we use a SimpleStories corpus of short, synthetically generated stories. Each story is written in simplified English with a small vocabulary and constrained syntax.

We treat this corpus as a causal language modeling benchmark. The text is tokenized with a subword tokenizer shared across all architectures, and training sequences are formed by concatenating stories and splitting them into fixed-length segments.
The model predicts the next token at each position using a left-to-right mask. We report validation perplexity.

Table 2
:
SimpleStories test results (mean
\pm
std over 2 seeds).

Model

Perplexity
\downarrow

Top-1 acc
\uparrow

Top-5 acc
\uparrow

Transformer

7.6701\pm 0.0313

50.441\pm 0.059
%

78.497\pm 0.062
%

Mamba2

7.7229\pm 0.0207

%

78.302\pm 0.043
%

Sessa

8.3700\pm 0.0482

49.144\pm 0.081
%

77.119\pm 0.090
%

We hypothesize that the small performance drop of Sessa in the short-context regime is due to the feedback mechanism being less necessary for this task.
Under matched parameter count, a portion of Sessa’s capacity is allocated to the feedback branch, which may be weakly utilized on short-context.
To test this interpretation, we ran a control experiment with the feedback branch removed while keeping the remainder of the architecture unchanged.
The ablated model improves over full Sessa on SimpleStories, reducing test perplexity from
8.3700\pm 0.0482
to
8.0902\pm 0.0192
and increasing top-1 accuracy from
49.144\pm 0.081
% to
49.648\pm 0.026
%.
This supports the view that feedback is less beneficial in the short-context regime, while remaining consistent with Sessa’s stronger results on long-context tasks, where feedback appears to be more useful.

6
Discussion

The main comparison in this paper is not between favorable operating regimes of Transformers, Mamba, and Sessa, but between matched regimes in which sharp retrieval is unavailable. For attention, this appears as diffuse, low-separation routing, so the selector cannot concentrate mass on a small set of relevant indices.
For Mamba, the analogous failure is failed freeze time, so the model cannot maintain a long preserve corridor on the relevant interval. These are natural failure regimes for the respective architectures, and they provide a common basis for comparison.

In this matched setting, the difference comes from the memory mechanism rather than from access to sharp routing. Diffuse attention remains one-hop and therefore suffers dilution.
Failed-freeze-time Mamba remains chain-structured and therefore exhibits exponential attenuation. Sessa is also studied in a diffuse regime, but its feedback solve aggregates influence over multiple hop counts and, in dense settings, over many temporal paths.
This is the structural source of its slower long-range decay.

The main separation is not only in the polynomial tail, but in the selective-retrieval result.
In the same family-over-
H
regime, deep Sessa realizes flexible selective retrieval profiles, whereas diffuse fixed-depth Transformers and failed-freeze-time fixed-depth Mamba do not realize frozen or increasing profiles.
Thus the separation is not merely quantitative at the level of decay rates; it is qualitative at the level of what retrieval behavior the architectures can realize under the same matched breakdown of sharp retrieval.

The broader point is that long-context behavior depends not only on how routing coefficients are produced, but also on how they are composed over time.
When sharp retrieval fails, as can become increasingly likely as context length grows, this distinction becomes decisive. In that regime, Sessa can still support flexible selective retrieval through its multi-hop feedback structure.

Appendix

Appendix A
Definitions and notation

A.1
Sequence norms and bounded-input sets

Definition 7

(Sup–
\ell_{2}
norm and bounded-input balls)
.

Fix a horizon
T\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and token width
D\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
For a finite sequence
x=(x_{0},\dots,x_{T-1})\in(\mathbb{R}^{D})^{T}
define

\|x\|_{\infty,2}:=\max_{0\leq t\leq T-1}\|x_{t}\|_{2}.

For
R\geq 0
define the ball

\mathcal{X}_{R}:=\{x\in(\mathbb{R}^{D})^{T}:\ \|x\|_{\infty,2}\leq R\}.

For infinite sequences
(x_{t})_{t\geq 0}
we use the analogous norm

\|x\|_{\infty,2}:=\sup_{t\geq 0}\|x_{t}\|_{2}\in[0,\infty]
.

\|X\|_{\infty,2}\leq\|X\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{T}\,\|X\|_{\infty,2}\qquad\text{for }X\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times D}.

(35)

A.2
BIBO stability on
\ell_{\infty}

Definition 8

(BIBO stability on
\ell_{\infty}
)
.

A map
\mathcal{N}:\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}^{D})\to\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}^{D})

is BIBO-stable with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,2}
if for every
B\geq 0
there exists

C_{B}<\infty
such that

\|x\|_{\infty,2}\leq B\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|\mathcal{N}(x)\|_{\infty,2}\leq C_{B}.

Appendix B
Jacobian tails under diffuse feedback routing

B.1
Sessa feedback solve as a parametric linear system

Fix a horizon
T\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and token width
D\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
Let
x=(x_{0},\dots,x_{T-1})\in(\mathbb{R}^{D})^{T}
be the input token sequence.
Let
f(x)=(f_{0}(x),\dots,f_{T-1}(x))\in(\mathbb{R}^{r})^{T}
be the forward sequence, where
r
is the value space dimension,
and let
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}(x)=(\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}(x))_{0\leq j<t\leq T-1}
be the strictly-lower attention weights.
Let
\gamma(x)=(\gamma_{0}(x),\dots,\gamma_{T-1}(x))
be the feedback gains.

Define the strictly lower-triangular matrix
B_{\mathrm{fb}}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times T}
by

\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,j}(x)=\begin{cases}\gamma_{t}(x)\,\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}(x),&j<t,\\
0,&j\geq t.\end{cases}

The mixer output
s(x)=(s_{0}(x),\dots,s_{T-1}(x))\in(\mathbb{R}^{r})^{T}
is defined as the unique solution to the causal solve

(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}}(x))\,s(x)=f(x).

(36)

Equivalently, by forward substitution,

s_{0}=f_{0},\qquad s_{t}=f_{t}+\gamma_{t}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}s_{j},\quad t\geq 1.

(37)

We measure long-range sensitivity by the Jacobian blocks

J_{t,\tau}(x):=\frac{\partial s_{t}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times D},\qquad 0\leq\tau\leq t\leq T-1.

Throughout this appendix we focus on the long-range case
\tau<t
and lag
\ell:=t-\tau\geq 1
.

B.2
Assumptions for diffuse routing and smoothness

Fix a radius
R\geq 0
and work on the ball
\mathcal{X}_{R}
from Definition
7
.

Remark B.1

(On the use of
t+1
and
t
in dilution bounds)
.

In this appendix the feedback attention is strictly-lower, meaning that
j<t
, so
|\mathcal{W}_{t}|=t
for
t\geq 1
.
We write
O(1/(t+1))
to avoid a special case at
t=0
and to match harmonic-series bounds;
for
t\geq 1
this is equivalent to
O(1/t)
up to absolute constants.

Assumption 15

(Row-stochasticity and diffuse envelope of feedback attention)
.

For every
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
and every
t\geq 1
,

\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}(x)\geq 0,\qquad\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}(x)=1,\qquad\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}(x)\leq\frac{c_{2}}{t}\quad\forall j<t,

for some constant
c_{2}=c_{2}(R)\in(0,\infty)
.
We set
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{0,\cdot}\equiv 0
.

Assumption 16

(Bounded feedback gain and nontrivial diffuse regime)
.

For every
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
and every
t
,

|\gamma_{t}(x)|\leq\gamma_{\max}<1,

and the diffuse feedback mass satisfies

\eta:=\gamma_{\max}c_{2}<1,\qquad\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}:=1-\eta\in(0,1).

Assumption 17

(Token-wise local feedback gain)
.

On
\mathcal{X}_{R}
, the feedback gain is token-wise: for each
t
one has
\gamma_{t}(x)=\gamma(x_{t})
.
In particular, for
\tau<t
,

\frac{\partial\gamma_{t}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}=0.

Assume additionally the token-wise Jacobian is bounded:

\Big\|\frac{\partial\gamma(x_{t})}{\partial x_{t}}\Big\|_{2}\leq L_{\gamma}\qquad\text{for all }\|x_{t}\|_{2}\leq R.

Assumption 18

(Causality of forward branch and routing)
.

For each time
k
, the quantities
f_{k}(x)
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{k,\cdot}(x)
, and
\gamma_{k}(x)
depend only on the prefix
x_{0:k}
.
Equivalently, for any
\tau>k
,

\frac{\partial f_{k}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}=0,\qquad\frac{\partial\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{k,j}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}=0\ \ (\forall j<k),\qquad\frac{\partial\gamma_{k}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}=0.

Assumption 19

(Local, same-token smoothness bounds)
.

There exist finite constants
L_{f,0}=L_{f,0}(R)
and
L_{\alpha,0}=L_{\alpha,0}(R)
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
and all
t
,

\Big\|\frac{\partial f_{t}(x)}{\partial x_{t}}\Big\|_{2}\leq L_{f,0},\qquad\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\Big\|\frac{\partial\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}(x)}{\partial x_{t}}\Big\|_{2}\leq L_{\alpha,0}.

Assumption 20

(Bounded forward sequence)
.

There exists
F_{R}<\infty
such that

\|f(x)\|_{\infty,2}\leq F_{R}\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}.

Assumption 21

(Forward-branch dilution of cross-token Jacobians)
.

There exists
L_{f}=L_{f}(R)<\infty
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
,
all
t\geq\tau
, and all
\tau<t
,

\Big\|\frac{\partial f_{t}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\leq\frac{L_{f}}{t+1}.

Here
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is the operator norm of the matrix
\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
.

Assumption 22

(Smooth routing:
\alpha
-weighted logit sensitivity)
.

Let
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,\cdot}(x)=\mathrm{softmax}(\beth_{t,0}(x),\dots,\beth_{t,t-1}(x))
denote the feedback-attention row
at time
t
, over
j<t
, with pre-softmax logits
\beth_{t,i}(x)
that may depend on the full prefix
x_{0:t}
.
There exists
L_{\mathrm{route}}=L_{\mathrm{route}}(R)<\infty
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
and all
t>\tau\geq 0
,

\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,i}(x)\,\Big\|\frac{\partial\beth_{t,i}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\;\leq\;\frac{L_{\mathrm{route}}}{t+1}.

Consequently, by Lemma
B.4
,

\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\Big\|\frac{\partial\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\;\leq\;\frac{2L_{\mathrm{route}}}{t+1}.

Remark B.2

(When Assumption
22
holds)
.

If the feedback query is token-wise,
q_{t}=q(x_{t})
, then for
\tau<t
the dependence of
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,\cdot}
on
x_{\tau}

typically enters only through key-side logits involving
k_{\tau}
, so only a small subset of logits have nonzero

\partial\beth_{t,i}/\partial x_{\tau}
. In that case, Assumption
22
reduces to the corresponding
localized logit-sensitivity bound.
More generally, if
q_{t}
, or other components upstream of logits, has cross-token sensitivity, Assumption
22

requires that the resulting
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}
-weighted logit sensitivities still dilute as
O(1/(t+1))
on
\mathcal{X}_{R}
.

B.3
Auxiliary lemmas

Lemma B.3

(Bound on the mixer state)
.

Under Assumption
16
–
20
, for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
,

\|s(x)\|_{\infty,2}\leq S_{R}:=\frac{F_{R}}{1-\gamma_{\max}}.

Proof.

Since each
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,\cdot}
is a convex distribution and
|\gamma_{t}|\leq\gamma_{\max}
,

\|s_{t}\|_{2}\leq\|f_{t}\|_{2}+\gamma_{\max}\max_{j<t}\|s_{j}\|_{2}.

A standard induction on
\max_{k\leq t}\|s_{k}\|_{2}
yields

\|s\|_{\infty,2}\leq(1-\gamma_{\max})^{-1}\|f\|_{\infty,2}\leq(1-\gamma_{\max})^{-1}F_{R}
.
∎

Lemma B.4

(Softmax row derivative: total variation bound)
.

Let
\alpha=\mathrm{softmax}(\beth)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with logits
\beth\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
depending on a parameter
z
.
Then

\sum_{j}\Big\|\frac{\partial\alpha_{j}}{\partial z}\Big\|\;\leq\;2\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\,\Big\|\frac{\partial\beth_{i}}{\partial z}\Big\|_{2}.

Proof.

The softmax Jacobian satisfies
\partial\alpha_{j}/\partial\beth_{i}=\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{1}[j=i]-\alpha_{i})
.
Thus

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big|\frac{\partial\alpha_{j}}{\partial\beth_{i}}\Big|=2\alpha_{i}(1-\alpha_{i})\leq 2\alpha_{i}.

By the chain rule,

\sum_{j}\|\partial\alpha_{j}/\partial z\|\leq\sum_{i}(\sum_{j}|\partial\alpha_{j}/\partial\beth_{i}|)\,\|\partial\beth_{i}/\partial z\|\leq 2\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\|\partial\beth_{i}/\partial z\|
.
∎

Lemma B.5

(Polynomial tail of the inverse kernel entries)
.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
and let
K(x):=(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}}(x))^{-1}
.
Under Assumptions
15
–
16
, there exists a constant

C_{K}:=\eta e^{\eta}=(1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}})e^{1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}

such that for all
0\leq k<t\leq T-1
,

|K_{t,k}(x)|\leq C_{K}\,(t-k)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad K_{t,t}(x)=1.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
, and abbreviate

B_{\mathrm{fb}}:=B_{\mathrm{fb}}(x),\qquad\alpha_{t,j}:=\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}(x),\qquad K:=K(x)=(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})^{-1}.

Since
B_{\mathrm{fb}}
is strictly lower-triangular on the finite horizon

\{0,\dots,T-1\}
, one has
B_{\mathrm{fb}}^{T}=0
, hence

K=(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})^{-1}=\sum_{m=0}^{T-1}B_{\mathrm{fb}}^{m}.

Therefore
K
is lower-triangular with unit diagonal:

K_{t,t}=1,\qquad K_{t,k}=0\ \text{for }t<k.

It remains to prove the off-diagonal estimate.

Fix a source index
k\in\{0,\dots,T-1\}
, and define

u_{t}:=|K_{t,k}|\qquad(t\geq k).

Then
u_{k}=|K_{k,k}|=1
. Also, since
(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})K=I
, equivalently

K=I+B_{\mathrm{fb}}K
, for every
t>k
we have

K_{t,k}=\sum_{j<t}\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,j}\,K_{j,k}.

Because
K_{j,k}=0
for
j<k
, this reduces to

K_{t,k}=\sum_{j=k}^{t-1}\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,j}\,K_{j,k}=\gamma_{t}(x)\sum_{j=k}^{t-1}\alpha_{t,j}\,K_{j,k}.

Taking absolute values and using Assumption
16
,

u_{t}\leq|\gamma_{t}(x)|\sum_{j=k}^{t-1}\alpha_{t,j}\,u_{j}\leq\gamma_{\max}\sum_{j=k}^{t-1}\alpha_{t,j}\,u_{j},\qquad t>k.

We now compare
u
to an explicit impulse-response sequence.
Define
(v_{t}^{(k)})_{t\geq 0}
by

v_{t}^{(k)}:=\begin{cases}0,&t<k,\\[5.69054pt]
1,&t=k,\\[5.69054pt]
\gamma_{\max}\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\widetilde{\alpha}_{t,j}\,v_{j}^{(k)},&t>k,\end{cases}

where the coefficients
\widetilde{\alpha}_{t,j}
are the following extension of the
finite-horizon row weights:

\widetilde{\alpha}_{t,j}:=\begin{cases}\alpha_{t,j},&0\leq j<t\leq T-1,\\[5.69054pt]
0,&t\geq T,\ 0\leq j<t.\end{cases}

Then
\widetilde{\alpha}_{t,j}\geq 0
,
\sum_{j<t}\widetilde{\alpha}_{t,j}\leq 1
for every

t\geq 1
, and by Assumption
15
,

\widetilde{\alpha}_{t,j}\leq\frac{c_{2}}{t}\qquad(t\geq 1,\ 0\leq j<t).

Thus the scalar recursion defining
v^{(k)}
satisfies the hypotheses of
Corollary
E.4
with impulse position
j=k
, attention envelope
constant
c_{2}
, and feedback bound
\gamma_{\max}
. In particular, with

\eta:=\gamma_{\max}c_{2},\qquad\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}:=1-\eta\in(0,1),

that corollary yields

v_{t}^{(k)}\leq\eta e^{\eta}\,(t-k)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\qquad\text{for all }t>k.

It remains to show that
u_{t}\leq v_{t}^{(k)}
for all
t\in\{k,\dots,T-1\}
.
We prove this by induction on
t
.

For
t=k
, one has
u_{k}=1=v_{k}^{(k)}
.

Now let
t>k
, and assume
u_{j}\leq v_{j}^{(k)}
for every
j\in\{k,\dots,t-1\}
.
Using (
B.3
), the nonnegativity of the coefficients
\alpha_{t,j}
,
and the induction hypothesis, we obtain

u_{t}\leq\gamma_{\max}\sum_{j=k}^{t-1}\alpha_{t,j}\,u_{j}\leq\gamma_{\max}\sum_{j=k}^{t-1}\alpha_{t,j}\,v_{j}^{(k)}.

Since
v_{j}^{(k)}=0
for
j<k
and
\widetilde{\alpha}_{t,j}=\alpha_{t,j}
for

t\leq T-1
, this is exactly

u_{t}\leq\gamma_{\max}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\widetilde{\alpha}_{t,j}\,v_{j}^{(k)}=v_{t}^{(k)}.

This closes the induction.

Combining the comparison
u_{t}\leq v_{t}^{(k)}
with
(
B.3
), we conclude that for every
0\leq k<t\leq T-1
,

|K_{t,k}(x)|=u_{t}\leq v_{t}^{(k)}\leq\eta e^{\eta}\,(t-k)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

Thus the claim holds with

C_{K}:=\eta e^{\eta}=(1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}})e^{\,1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

Together with
K_{t,t}=1
, this proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma B.6

(A convolution bound)
.

Let
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}\in(0,1)
. There exists
C_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}<\infty
such that for all integers
\ell\geq 1
and all
\tau\geq 0
,

\sum_{k=\tau}^{\tau+\ell-1}\frac{1}{(\tau+\ell-k)^{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}}\cdot\frac{1}{k+1}\ \leq\ C_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\big(1+\log(1+\ell)\big).

One may take, for instance,

C_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}:=2^{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\,+\,\frac{2^{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}}{1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

Proof.

Write
k=\tau+m
where
m=0,\dots,\ell-1
:

\sum_{m=0}^{\ell-1}\frac{1}{(\ell-m)^{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\tau+m+1}.

Split into
m\leq\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor
and
m>\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor
.

If
m\leq\ell/2
, then
(\ell-m)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\leq(\ell/2)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}=2^{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}
and

\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}\frac{1}{\tau+m+1}\leq 1+\int_{0}^{\ell/2}\frac{dm}{\tau+m+1}\leq 1+\log(1+\ell).

Thus this part is
\leq 2^{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}(1+\log(1+\ell))
.

If
m>\ell/2
, then
\tau+m+1\geq\ell/2
, so
(\tau+m+1)^{-1}\leq 2/\ell
, hence

\sum_{m>\ell/2}\frac{1}{(\ell-m)^{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\tau+m+1}\leq\frac{2}{\ell}\sum_{r=1}^{\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}\frac{1}{r^{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}}\leq\frac{2}{\ell}\Big(1+\int_{1}^{\ell/2}r^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\,dr\Big)\leq\frac{2}{\ell}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)^{1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}=\frac{2^{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}}{1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

Combine the two bounds.
∎

B.4
Polynomial Jacobian tail

Theorem 23

(Polynomial Jacobian tail under diffuse routing)
.

Assume Assumptions
15
–
22
,
17
,

18
, and
19
hold on
\mathcal{X}_{R}
, and let

\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}:=1-\gamma_{\max}c_{2}\in(0,1)
as in Assumption
16
.
Then there exists a constant
C(R)<\infty
such that for every
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
and every pair
\tau<t
with lag
\ell=t-\tau\geq 1
,

\Big\|\frac{\partial s_{t}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\ \leq\ C(R)\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\big(1+\log(1+\ell)\big).

In particular, long-range sensitivity decays at least polynomially in the lag, up to a logarithmic factor.

One may take explicitly

C(R):=\widetilde{C}_{K}\Big(A_{0}(R)+(1+C_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}})\,A_{1}(R)\Big),\quad\widetilde{C}_{K}:=\max\{1,C_{K}\},\quad C_{K}=\eta e^{\eta},\quad\eta=\gamma_{\max}c_{2},

where
C_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}
is as in Lemma
B.6
and

A_{1}(R):=L_{f}+2\gamma_{\max}\,S_{R}\,L_{\mathrm{route}},\qquad A_{0}(R):=L_{f,0}+L_{\gamma}\,S_{R}+\gamma_{\max}\,S_{R}\,L_{\alpha,0},\qquad S_{R}=\frac{F_{R}}{1-\gamma_{\max}}.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
and a source index
\tau
.
Differentiate the solve (
36
) with respect to
x_{\tau}
:

(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})\,\frac{\partial s}{\partial x_{\tau}}-\frac{\partial B_{\mathrm{fb}}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\,s=\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{\tau}}.

Multiplying by
K=(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})^{-1}
gives

\frac{\partial s}{\partial x_{\tau}}=K\Big(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{\tau}}+\frac{\partial B_{\mathrm{fb}}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\,s\Big).

(38)

Taking the
t
-th row and operator norms yields

\Big\|\frac{\partial s_{t}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{t}|K_{t,k}|\cdot\Big\|\frac{\partial f_{k}}{\partial x_{\tau}}+\big(\frac{\partial B_{\mathrm{fb}}}{\partial x_{\tau}}s\big)_{k}\Big\|_{2}.

(39)

By Assumption
18
, if
k<\tau
then
\partial f_{k}/\partial x_{\tau}=0
and

\partial B_{\mathrm{fb},k,\cdot}/\partial x_{\tau}=0
, hence the sum starts at
k=\tau
.

Bounding the forcing term.

We treat the single index
k=\tau
separately from the range
k>\tau
.

Case 1:
k>\tau
.

For
k>\tau
, Assumption
21
gives

\Big\|\frac{\partial f_{k}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\leq\frac{L_{f}}{k+1}.

It remains to bound
\|(\partial B_{\mathrm{fb}}/\partial x_{\tau})s\|
.
For
k>\tau
we use the full decomposition

\frac{\partial\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{k,j}}{\partial x_{\tau}}=\frac{\partial\gamma_{k}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\,\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{k,j}+\gamma_{k}\,\frac{\partial\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{k,j}}{\partial x_{\tau}}.

By Assumption
17
,
\partial\gamma_{k}/\partial x_{\tau}=0
for
k>\tau
, so only the second term remains.
Therefore, using Lemma
B.3
and Assumption
22
,

\Big\|\big(\frac{\partial B_{\mathrm{fb}}}{\partial x_{\tau}}s\big)_{k}\Big\|_{2}\leq|\gamma_{k}|\sum_{j<k}\Big\|\frac{\partial\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{k,j}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\cdot\|s_{j}\|_{2}\leq\gamma_{\max}\,S_{R}\sum_{j<k}\Big\|\frac{\partial\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{k,j}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\leq\gamma_{\max}\,S_{R}\cdot\frac{2L_{\mathrm{route}}}{k+1}.

Thus for all
k>\tau
,

\Big\|\frac{\partial f_{k}}{\partial x_{\tau}}+\big(\frac{\partial B_{\mathrm{fb}}}{\partial x_{\tau}}s\big)_{k}\Big\|_{2}\leq\frac{A_{1}(R)}{k+1},\qquad A_{1}(R):=L_{f}+2\gamma_{\max}\,S_{R}\,L_{\mathrm{route}}.

Case 2:
k=\tau
.

Using Assumption
19
and Lemma
B.3
, we bound

\Big\|\frac{\partial f_{\tau}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\leq L_{f,0}.

Moreover, since
\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{\tau,j}=\gamma_{\tau}\,\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{\tau,j}
for
j<\tau
,

\Big\|\big(\tfrac{\partial B_{\mathrm{fb}}}{\partial x_{\tau}}s\big)_{\tau}\Big\|_{2}\leq\Big\|\tfrac{\partial\gamma_{\tau}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\cdot\sum_{j<\tau}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{\tau,j}\|s_{j}\|_{2}\;+\;|\gamma_{\tau}|\sum_{j<\tau}\Big\|\tfrac{\partial\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{\tau,j}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\cdot\|s_{j}\|_{2}\leq L_{\gamma}\,S_{R}+\gamma_{\max}\,L_{\alpha,0}\,S_{R}.

Hence

\Big\|\frac{\partial f_{\tau}}{\partial x_{\tau}}+\big(\frac{\partial B_{\mathrm{fb}}}{\partial x_{\tau}}s\big)_{\tau}\Big\|_{2}\leq A_{0}(R),\qquad A_{0}(R):=L_{f,0}+L_{\gamma}\,S_{R}+\gamma_{\max}\,S_{R}\,L_{\alpha,0}.

Kernel tail and convolution.

Plugging the forcing bound into (
39
) and using Lemma
B.5
yields

\Big\|\frac{\partial s_{t}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\leq\ |K_{t,\tau}|\,A_{0}(R)\ +\ \sum_{k=\tau+1}^{t}|K_{t,k}|\cdot\frac{A_{1}(R)}{k+1}\leq|K_{t,\tau}|\,A_{0}(R)\ +\ A_{1}(R)\Big(\frac{1}{t+1}+\sum_{k=\tau+1}^{t-1}C_{K}\,(t-k)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\cdot\frac{1}{k+1}\Big).

Let
\ell=t-\tau\geq 1
.

We keep the
k=t
term explicit and show it can be absorbed into the final tail factor:

\frac{1}{t+1}\leq\frac{1}{\tau+\ell+1}\leq\frac{1}{\ell+1}\leq\ell^{-1}.

Since
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}\in(0,1)
and
\ell\geq 1
, we have
\ell^{1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\geq 1
, hence

\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}=\ell^{1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\,\ell^{-1}\ \geq\ \ell^{-1}.

Therefore,

\frac{1}{t+1}\ \leq\ \ell^{-1}\ \leq\ \ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\ \leq\ \ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\big(1+\log(1+\ell)\big),

(40)

so the
k=t
contribution
\frac{A_{1}(R)}{t+1}
is dominated by the same

\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}(1+\log(1+\ell))
envelope, with constant
1
.

For the isolated term, Lemma
B.5
gives
|K_{t,\tau}|\leq C_{K}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}
.
For the remaining sum, apply Lemma
B.6

Note that
\sum_{k=\tau+1}^{t-1}\leq\sum_{k=\tau}^{t-1}
:

\sum_{k=\tau}^{t-1}(t-k)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\cdot\frac{1}{k+1}\leq C_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}(1+\log(1+\ell)).

Therefore

\Big\|\frac{\partial s_{t}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\leq C_{K}\,A_{0}(R)\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\ +\ A_{1}(R)\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}(1+\log(1+\ell))\ +\ C_{K}\,C_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\,A_{1}(R)\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}(1+\log(1+\ell)).

Since
\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\leq\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}(1+\log(1+\ell))
for
\ell\geq 1
and

\widetilde{C}_{K}=\max\{1,C_{K}\}\geq 1
and
\widetilde{C}_{K}\geq C_{K}
, we obtain

\Big\|\frac{\partial s_{t}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\leq\widetilde{C}_{K}\Big(A_{0}(R)+(1+C_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}})A_{1}(R)\Big)\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}(1+\log(1+\ell)),

which is the claim with the stated
C(R)
.
∎

B.5
Jacobian tail for block outputs

Consider the simplified block output of the form

y_{t}=x_{t}+W^{\mathrm{out}}\,(s_{t}\odot g_{t})+b^{\mathrm{out}},

where
g_{t}=g_{t}(x_{t})
is token-wise and serves as a gate, and
W^{\mathrm{out}}
is a fixed matrix.

Corollary B.7

(Jacobian tail for block outputs)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
23
, suppose additionally that

\|g(x)\|_{\infty,2}\leq G_{R}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
. Then for every
\tau<t
with lag
\ell=t-\tau\geq 1
,

\Big\|\frac{\partial y_{t}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\ \leq\ \|W^{\mathrm{out}}\|_{2}\,G_{R}\cdot C(R)\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}(1+\log(1+\ell)),\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}.

Proof.

For
\tau<t
,
\partial x_{t}/\partial x_{\tau}=0
, and since
g_{t}
is token-wise,
\partial g_{t}/\partial x_{\tau}=0
.
Thus

\frac{\partial y_{t}}{\partial x_{\tau}}=W^{\mathrm{out}}\,\mathrm{Diag}(g_{t})\,\frac{\partial s_{t}}{\partial x_{\tau}}.

Taking operator norms and using
\|\mathrm{Diag}(g_{t})\|_{2}\leq\|g_{t}\|_{2}\leq G_{R}
plus Theorem
23

gives the result.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs for Section
4.2

Lemma C.1

(Bounded logit spread implies near-uniform softmax weights)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a finite index set with
n:=|\mathcal{I}|
, and let
(\beth_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{I}}\subset\mathbb{R}
be logits.
Define the softmax weights

\alpha_{j}\;=\;\frac{e^{\beth_{j}}}{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}e^{\beth_{i}}},\qquad j\in\mathcal{I}.

If the logit spread is bounded by

\Delta\;:=\;\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\beth_{i}\;-\;\min_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\beth_{i}\;\leq\;\Delta_{0}<\infty,

then for every
j\in\mathcal{I}
,

\frac{e^{-\Delta_{0}}}{n}\;\leq\;\alpha_{j}\;\leq\;\frac{e^{\Delta_{0}}}{n}.

(41)

Equivalently, for all
i,j\in\mathcal{I}
one has
e^{-\Delta_{0}}\leq\alpha_{i}/\alpha_{j}\leq e^{\Delta_{0}}
.
In particular, if
\Delta_{0}
is uniformly bounded while
n
grows, then
\alpha_{j}=\Theta(1/n)
uniformly over
j\in\mathcal{I}
.

Proof.

Let
\beth_{\min}:=\min_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\beth_{i}
. Then
\beth_{\min}\leq\beth_{j}\leq\beth_{\min}+\Delta_{0}
for all
j\in\mathcal{I}
, hence

e^{\beth_{\min}}\leq e^{\beth_{j}}\leq e^{\beth_{\min}+\Delta_{0}}
and

n\,e^{\beth_{\min}}\;\leq\;\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}e^{\beth_{i}}\;\leq\;n\,e^{\beth_{\min}+\Delta_{0}}.

Dividing
e^{\beth_{j}}
by these bounds yields (
41
).
∎

C.1
Proof of Lemma
4.3

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

Fix a time
t
and an index
\tau<t
. Write

\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,\cdot}(x)=\Softmax(\beth_{t,0}(x),\dots,\beth_{t,t}(x)),\qquad\alpha_{j}:=\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,j}(x),\quad\beta_{j}:=\beth_{t,j}(x),\qquad 0\leq j\leq t.

Thus
\alpha=\Softmax(\beta)\in\mathbb{R}^{t+1}
and
\sum_{j\leq t}\alpha_{j}=1
.

Recall the standard softmax Jacobian identity: for all
j,i\in\{0,\dots,t\}
, the softmax partial derivatives satisfy

\frac{\partial\alpha_{j}}{\partial\beta_{i}}=\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{1}[j=i]-\alpha_{i}).

(42)

By assumption, for each
j\leq t
,

\beta_{j}=\beth_{t,j}(x)=\langle q(x_{t}),\,k(x_{j})\rangle,

where
q,k
are token-wise maps. Since
\tau<t
, the quantity
q(x_{t})
depends only on
x_{t}
, hence

\partial q(x_{t})/\partial x_{\tau}=0
. Similarly,
k(x_{j})
depends only on
x_{j}
, hence

\partial k(x_{j})/\partial x_{\tau}=0
unless
j=\tau
. Therefore,

\frac{\partial\beta_{i}}{\partial x_{\tau}}=0\quad\text{for all }i\neq\tau,\qquad\text{and potentially}\qquad\frac{\partial\beta_{\tau}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\neq 0.

(43)

Consequently, by the chain rule and (
43
),

\frac{\partial\alpha_{j}}{\partial x_{\tau}}=\sum_{i\leq t}\frac{\partial\alpha_{j}}{\partial\beta_{i}}\frac{\partial\beta_{i}}{\partial x_{\tau}}=\frac{\partial\alpha_{j}}{\partial\beta_{\tau}}\frac{\partial\beta_{\tau}}{\partial x_{\tau}}=\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{1}[j=\tau]-\alpha_{\tau})\,\frac{\partial\beta_{\tau}}{\partial x_{\tau}},

where we used (
42
) in the last step. Taking operator norms gives

\Big\|\frac{\partial\alpha_{j}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}=\big|\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{1}[j=\tau]-\alpha_{\tau})\big|\cdot\Big\|\frac{\partial\beta_{\tau}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}.

(44)

Summing (
44
) over
j\leq t
yields

\sum_{j\leq t}\Big\|\frac{\partial\alpha_{j}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}=\Big(\sum_{j\leq t}\big|\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{1}[j=\tau]-\alpha_{\tau})\big|\Big)\Big\|\frac{\partial\beta_{\tau}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}.

To evaluate the scalar sum, note that

\sum_{j\leq t}\big|\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{1}[j=\tau]-\alpha_{\tau})\big|=\underbrace{\alpha_{\tau}(1-\alpha_{\tau})}_{j=\tau}+\underbrace{\sum_{j\neq\tau}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{\tau}}_{j\neq\tau}=\alpha_{\tau}(1-\alpha_{\tau})+\alpha_{\tau}\sum_{j\neq\tau}\alpha_{j}=2\alpha_{\tau}(1-\alpha_{\tau})\leq 2\alpha_{\tau},

since
\sum_{j\neq\tau}\alpha_{j}=1-\alpha_{\tau}
and
1-\alpha_{\tau}\leq 1
.
Therefore,

\sum_{j\leq t}\Big\|\frac{\partial\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,j}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\leq 2\,\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,\tau}(x)\,\Big\|\frac{\partial\beth_{t,\tau}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2},

which is the first claim.

In particular.

If
\|\partial\beth_{t,\tau}(x)/\partial x_{\tau}\|_{2}\leq L_{\beth}
on
\mathcal{X}_{R}
, then

\sum_{j\leq t}\Big\|\frac{\partial\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,j}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|_{2}\leq 2L_{\beth}\,\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,\tau}(x).

In the diffuse regime of Definition
4
, Lemma
C.1
implies

\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,\tau}(x)=\Theta(1/|\mathcal{W}_{t}|)
uniformly over
\tau\in\mathcal{W}_{t}
, hence the right-hand side is

\lesssim 1/|\mathcal{W}_{t}|
. For full-prefix attention
|\mathcal{W}_{t}|=t+1
.
∎

C.2
Proof of Proposition
9

Proof of Proposition
9
.

Fix a horizon
T
and work with the fixed-routing Jacobians from
Section
4.2.1
.

(1) Transformer: attention one-hop dilution.

By definition of the value influence Jacobian under realized attention weights, by Eq. (
26
),

J^{\mathrm{attn}}_{t,\tau}=\frac{\partial y_{t}}{\partial v_{\tau}}\Big|_{\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}}=\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,\tau}\,I.

Taking operator norms and using
\|I\|=1
gives

\|J^{\mathrm{attn}}_{t,\tau}\|=\|\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,\tau}I\|=\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,\tau}.

Assume the shared diffuse (low-separation) regime of Definition
4
with
full-prefix visibility
\mathcal{W}_{t}=\{0,\dots,t\}
, so
|\mathcal{W}_{t}|=t+1
.
The bounded logit spread over
\mathcal{W}_{t}
implies, by Lemma
C.1
, that for every
\tau\leq t
,

\frac{e^{-\Delta}}{t+1}\leq\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,\tau}\leq\frac{e^{\Delta}}{t+1},

hence
\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{t,\tau}=\Theta(1/(t+1))
and therefore

\|J^{\mathrm{attn}}_{t,\tau}\|=\Theta\!\Big(\frac{1}{t+1}\Big)\qquad(\tau\leq t).

For a fixed old source
\tau=O(1)
and lag
\ell=t-\tau
, we have

\|J^{\mathrm{attn}}_{\tau+\ell,\tau}\|=\alpha^{\mathrm{fwd}}_{\tau+\ell,\tau}=\Theta\!\Big(\frac{1}{\tau+\ell+1}\Big)=\Theta(1/\ell),

since
\tau
is fixed and
\ell\to\infty
.

(2) Mamba under failed freeze time.

By definition of the fixed-routing impulse Jacobian for an SSM, by Eq. (
28
),

J^{\mathrm{ssm}}_{t,\tau}={C}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}\Big(\prod_{r=\tau+1}^{t}{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},r}\Big){B}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau},\qquad 0\leq\tau\leq t.

Assume the realized recurrence has diagonal transitions

{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},r}=\operatorname{diag}(\exp(-a_{n}\Delta_{r})),\qquad a_{n}\geq\lambda>0,

and bounded input/output factors

\sup_{r}\|{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},r}\|\leq B_{\max},\qquad\sup_{r}\|{C}_{\mathrm{ssm},r}\|\leq C_{\max}.

Then

\Big\|\prod_{r=\tau+1}^{t}{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},r}\Big\|=\max_{n}\exp\!\Big(-a_{n}\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}\Big)\leq\exp\!\Big(-\lambda\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}\Big).

Under the failed-freeze-time condition

\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}\geq c_{\Delta}(t-\tau),

it follows that

\|J^{\mathrm{ssm}}_{t,\tau}\|\leq C_{\max}B_{\max}\exp\!\big(-\lambda c_{\Delta}(t-\tau)\big).

Setting
c:=C_{\max}B_{\max}
and
\ell:=t-\tau
gives

\|J^{\mathrm{ssm}}_{t,\tau}\|\leq c\,e^{-\lambda c_{\Delta}\ell}.

(3) Sessa: diffuse feedback routing.

For a realized feedback matrix
B_{\mathrm{fb}}
, the solve Jacobian is the resolvent given by Eq. (
27
)

J^{\mathrm{sessa}}=(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})^{-1},\qquad J^{\mathrm{sessa}}_{t,\tau}=[(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})^{-1}]_{t,\tau}.

Since
B_{\mathrm{fb}}
is scalar-valued,
J^{\mathrm{sessa}}_{t,\tau}\in\mathbb{R}
is a scalar coefficient shared across features.

Fix
\tau
and consider the impulse in the forward stream
f
at time
\tau
:

f_{\tau}=1
and
f_{t}=0
for
t\neq\tau
. Let
s
be the solution to
(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})s=f
.
By linearity,
s_{t}=J^{\mathrm{sessa}}_{t,\tau}
for all
t
.
Moreover, by forward substitution (equivalently (
31
)),
s_{\tau}=1
and for
t>\tau
,

s_{t}=f_{t}+\gamma_{t}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}s_{j}=\gamma_{t}\sum_{j=\tau}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}s_{j},

since
f_{t}=0
for
t\neq\tau
and
s_{j}=0
for
j<\tau
in a strictly causal solve.

Under Assumptions
6
–
7
we have

\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}\leq c_{2}/t
for all
j<t
and
|\gamma_{t}|\leq\gamma_{\max}<1
, and defining

\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}:=1-\gamma_{\max}c_{2}\in(0,1]
, with
\gamma_{\max}c_{2}<1
,
Theorem
8
applies to this impulse recursion, shifted to start at
\tau
, and yields that for all lags
\ell\geq 1
,

|J^{\mathrm{sessa}}_{\tau+\ell,\tau}|=|s_{\tau+\ell}|\leq C\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}},

for an explicit constant
C
, e.g.
C=(1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}})e^{\,1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}
.

Tightness.

In the explicit uniform-routing regime

\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,j}=\begin{cases}0,&t=0,\\[2.0pt]
\dfrac{\gamma}{t}\mathbf{1}[j<t],&t\geq 1,\end{cases}\qquad\gamma\in(0,1),

one has
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}=t^{-1}\mathbf{1}[j<t]
and constant gain

\gamma_{t}\equiv\gamma
, hence
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}=1-\gamma
.
Appendix Corollary
F.2
gives, for every fixed source position
\tau
,

|J^{\mathrm{sessa}}_{\tau+\ell,\tau}|=\Theta_{\tau}(\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}).

Moreover, Appendix Corollary
F.3
yields the stronger
uniform statement that for every
\tau_{\max}<\infty
there exist constants

c^{-}_{\tau_{\max}},c^{+}_{\tau_{\max}}>0
such that

c^{-}_{\tau_{\max}}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\leq|J^{\mathrm{sessa}}_{\tau+\ell,\tau}|\leq c^{+}_{\tau_{\max}}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}

for all
0\leq\tau\leq\tau_{\max}
and all
\ell\geq 1
.
Thus the one-layer envelope is tight for each fixed source and uniformly on every bounded source family,
in particular on every fixed finite horizon.
∎

C.3
Proof of Proposition
3

Proof.

The claim is about the input–output map and is independent of the chosen realization.
By the controllable and observable decomposition, also known as the Kalman decomposition
(
2
)
, there exists a similarity transform that isolates the
controllable and observable subsystem
({A}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}},{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}},{C}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}})
such that for all
\ell\geq 0
,

{C}_{\mathrm{ssm}}{A}_{\mathrm{ssm}}^{\ell}{B}_{\mathrm{ssm}}\;=\;{C}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}}\,{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}}^{\ell}\,{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}}.

Moreover,
({A}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}},{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}},{C}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}})
is a minimal realization of the same transfer function, so it admits no pole–zero cancellations and its poles coincide with the reachable and observable eigenvalues of
{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}}

(
13
)
. Since the transfer function is BIBO stable, all its poles lie strictly inside the unit disk (DT case)
(
12
)
; hence
\rho_{\mathrm{spec}}\!\left({A}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}}\right)<1
.
It follows from standard finite-dimensional matrix power bounds that there exist
c>0
and
\kappa\in(0,1)
such that

\|{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}}^{\ell}\|\leq c\,\kappa^{\ell}
for all
\ell
, and therefore

\|{C}_{\mathrm{ssm}}{A}_{\mathrm{ssm}}^{\ell}{B}_{\mathrm{ssm}}\|=\|{C}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}}{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}}^{\ell}{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},\mathrm{co}}\|\leq c^{\prime}\kappa^{\ell}
.
∎

C.4
Proof of Proposition
4

The key point is that, under ZOH discretization, the state-transition product is controlled by the
accumulated discretization time

\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}(x),

since each channel contributes a factor
\exp(-a_{n}\Delta_{r}(x))
. Accordingly, the proof first obtains
an end-to-end Jacobian bound in terms of

\Pi_{t,\ell}(x)=\exp\!\Big(-\lambda\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}(x)\Big),

and only then converts this into exponential-in-lag decay under failed freeze time.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
and indices
\tau<t
, and set
\ell:=t-\tau\geq 1
.
Write
J^{h}_{t,\tau}:=\partial h_{t}(x)/\partial x_{\tau}
and
J^{\mathrm{e2e}}_{t,\tau}:=\partial y_{t}(x)/\partial x_{\tau}
.
We use the product convention

\prod_{r=\tau+1}^{t}{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},r}:={A}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},t-1}\cdots{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau+1},\qquad\prod_{r=t+1}^{t}(\cdot):=I.

State bound via ZOH convexity.

In a ZOH-diagonal channel, each mode
n
evolves as the scalar recursion

(h_{t})_{n}=e^{-a_{n}\Delta_{t}}\,(h_{t-1})_{n}+\frac{1-e^{-a_{n}\Delta_{t}}}{a_{n}}\,(b_{t})_{n},\qquad a_{n}\geq\lambda,\ \Delta_{t}\geq 0,

where we take

b_{t}:=\widetilde{{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}}(x_{t})u_{t}(x_{t}).

By the bounds on
\widetilde{{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}}
and
u_{t}
on
\mathcal{X}_{R}
, we have

\|b_{t}\|\leq G_{\max}U_{R},

and hence
|(b_{t})_{n}|\leq G_{\max}U_{R}
for each mode.
Since
h_{-1}=0
, Lemma
4.4
applied componentwise with
a_{\min}=\lambda
gives

\sup_{t}|(h_{t})_{n}|\leq\frac{G_{\max}U_{R}}{\lambda}\qquad\text{for every mode }n.

Therefore

\|h_{t}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{d_{\mathrm{state}}}\,\|h_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{d_{\mathrm{state}}}\,\frac{G_{\max}U_{R}}{\lambda}=:H_{R}.

Jacobian recursion for
t>\tau
.

For
t>\tau
, locality implies

\frac{\partial{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}(x_{t})}{\partial x_{\tau}}=\frac{\partial\widetilde{{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}}(x_{t})}{\partial x_{\tau}}=\frac{\partial u_{t}(x_{t})}{\partial x_{\tau}}=\frac{\partial{G}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}(x_{t})}{\partial x_{\tau}}=0.

Differentiating

h_{t}={A}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}(x_{t})\,h_{t-1}+{G}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}(x_{t})\,\widetilde{{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}}(x_{t})\,u_{t}(x_{t})

with respect to
x_{\tau}
yields

J^{h}_{t,\tau}={A}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}(x_{t})\,J^{h}_{t-1,\tau},\qquad t>\tau.

Iterating gives

J^{h}_{t,\tau}=\Big(\prod_{r=\tau+1}^{t}{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},r}(x_{r})\Big)\,J^{h}_{\tau,\tau}.

Source-time derivative bound.

At
t=\tau
, write
b_{\tau}:=\widetilde{{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau}}(x_{\tau})u_{\tau}(x_{\tau})
and differentiate the ZOH update:

J^{h}_{\tau,\tau}=\Big(\frac{\partial{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau}(x_{\tau})}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big)h_{\tau-1}+\Big(\frac{\partial{G}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau}(x_{\tau})}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big)b_{\tau}+{G}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau}(x_{\tau})\,\frac{\partial b_{\tau}}{\partial x_{\tau}}.

Moreover,

\frac{\partial b_{\tau}}{\partial x_{\tau}}=\Big(\frac{\partial\widetilde{{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau}}(x_{\tau})}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big)u_{\tau}+\widetilde{{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau}}(x_{\tau})\Big(\frac{\partial u_{\tau}(x_{\tau})}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big).

Since

{G}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau}(x_{\tau})=\operatorname{diag}\!\Big(\frac{1-[{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau}(x_{\tau})]_{n}}{a_{n}}\Big)_{n},

we have the operator bounds

\|{G}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau}(x_{\tau})\|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda},\qquad\Big\|\frac{\partial{G}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau}(x_{\tau})}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\Big\|\frac{\partial{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},\tau}(x_{\tau})}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|.

Using

\|h_{\tau-1}\|\leq H_{R},\qquad\|b_{\tau}\|\leq G_{\max}U_{R},

together with the derivative bounds gives

\|J^{h}_{\tau,\tau}\|\leq L_{A}\,H_{R}\;+\;\frac{L_{A}}{\lambda}\,G_{\max}U_{R}\;+\;\frac{1}{\lambda}\big(L_{B}\,U_{R}+G_{\max}\,L_{u}\big)=:J_{R}.

Transition product bound by accumulated discretization time.

Since each
{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},r}
is diagonal with entries
\exp(-a_{n}\Delta_{r})
and
a_{n}\geq\lambda
,

\Big\|\prod_{r=\tau+1}^{t}{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},r}(x_{r})\Big\|=\max_{n}\exp\!\Big(-a_{n}\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}(x)\Big)\leq\exp\!\Big(-\lambda\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}(x)\Big)=:\Pi_{t,\ell}(x).

Therefore

\|J^{h}_{t,\tau}\|\leq\Pi_{t,\ell}(x)\,\|J^{h}_{\tau,\tau}\|\leq J_{R}\,\Pi_{t,\ell}(x).

Output Jacobian.

For
\tau<t
, locality implies
\partial{C}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}(x_{t})/\partial x_{\tau}=0
, so

\frac{\partial y_{t}}{\partial x_{\tau}}={C}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}(x_{t})\,J^{h}_{t,\tau}.

Hence

\Big\|\frac{\partial y_{t}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|\leq\|{C}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}(x_{t})\|\,\|J^{h}_{t,\tau}\|\leq C_{R}\,J_{R}\,\Pi_{t,\ell}(x).

Thus the claim holds with

C(R):=C_{R}J_{R}.

∎

C.5
Proof of Lemma
4.4

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Fix
t\geq 0
and define
\theta_{t}:=e^{-a\Delta_{t}}\in[0,1]
, since
a>0
and
\Delta_{t}\geq 0
.
Then
1-\theta_{t}=1-e^{-a\Delta_{t}}\in[0,1]
, and the update can be rewritten as

h_{t}=\theta_{t}\,h_{t-1}+(1-\theta_{t})\,\frac{b_{t}}{a}.

Taking absolute values and using the triangle inequality yields

|h_{t}|\leq\theta_{t}|h_{t-1}|+(1-\theta_{t})\frac{|b_{t}|}{a}.

Since
\theta_{t}\in[0,1]
, for any
u,v\geq 0
one has
\theta_{t}u+(1-\theta_{t})v\leq\max\{u,v\}
, hence

|h_{t}|\leq\max\Big\{|h_{t-1}|,\ \frac{|b_{t}|}{a}\Big\}\leq\max\Big\{|h_{t-1}|,\ \frac{|b_{t}|}{a_{\min}}\Big\},

using
a\geq a_{\min}
.

Define

B_{t}:=\max\Big\{|h_{-1}|,\ \max_{0\leq s\leq t}\frac{|b_{s}|}{a_{\min}}\Big\}.

We claim by induction that
|h_{t}|\leq B_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
.
For
t=0
this follows from the previous inequality. If
|h_{t-1}|\leq B_{t-1}
, then

|h_{t}|\leq\max\Big\{|h_{t-1}|,\ \frac{|b_{t}|}{a_{\min}}\Big\}\leq\max\Big\{B_{t-1},\ \frac{|b_{t}|}{a_{\min}}\Big\}=B_{t},

proving the induction. Taking
\sup_{t\geq 0}
gives

\sup_{t\geq 0}|h_{t}|\leq\max\Big\{|h_{-1}|,\ \sup_{s\geq 0}\frac{|b_{s}|}{a_{\min}}\Big\},

which is the general bound.

If additionally
|b_{t}|\leq M
for all
t
and
h_{-1}=0
, then the right-hand side is at most
M/a_{\min}
,
proving
\sup_{t}|h_{t}|\leq M/a_{\min}
.
∎

Remark C.2

(Vector and diagonal case)
.

For diagonal
A=-\mathrm{diag}(a_{n})
with
\min_{n}a_{n}\geq a_{\min}
, the bound holds componentwise for each mode and channel,
and hence yields the uniform bound
\|h_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq\sup_{s}\|b_{s}\|_{\infty}/a_{\min}
.
More generally, for any monotone norm
\|\cdot\|
one has

\|h_{t}\|\leq\|\mathbf{1}\|\,\sup_{s}\|b_{s}\|_{\infty}/a_{\min}
.

C.6
Proof of Corollary
4.6

Proof.

Proposition
4
gives

\Big\|\frac{\partial y_{t}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|\leq C(R)\,\Pi_{t,\ell}(x),\qquad\Pi_{t,\ell}(x)=\exp\!\Big(-\lambda\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}(x)\Big).

Under failed freeze time,

\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}(x)\geq c_{\Delta}(t-\tau).

Applying Proposition
5
yields

\Pi_{t,\ell}(x)\leq\exp\!\big(-\lambda c_{\Delta}(t-\tau)\big),

and therefore

\Big\|\frac{\partial y_{t}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|\leq C(R)\exp\!\big(-\lambda c_{\Delta}(t-\tau)\big).

∎

Remark C.3

(Local windows)
.

If
{A}_{\mathrm{ssm},t},\widetilde{{B}_{\mathrm{ssm},t}},{C}_{\mathrm{ssm},t},u_{t}
depend on a fixed window
x_{t-K:t}
, the same argument yields

\Big\|\frac{\partial y_{t}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|\leq C(R)\,\exp\!\Big(-\lambda\sum_{r=\tau+K+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}(x)\Big)\qquad(t>\tau+K),

so the same failed-freeze-time conclusion holds up to a finite-window slack.

C.7
Proof of Proposition
5

Proof.

By definition,

\Pi_{t,\ell}=\exp\!\Big(-\lambda\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}\Big).

Under the failed-freeze-time condition

\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}\geq c_{\Delta}(t-\tau)=c_{\Delta}\ell,

we obtain

\Pi_{t,\ell}\leq\exp\!\big(-\lambda c_{\Delta}\ell\big).

This is exactly the claim.
∎

C.8
Details for Proposition
10

Proof.

(1) Transformer attention in the no-freeze setting.

Let
y_{t}(x)=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{W}_{t}}\alpha_{t,j}(x)\,v(x_{j})
.
For
\tau<t
, differentiate:

\frac{\partial y_{t}}{\partial x_{\tau}}=\alpha_{t,\tau}\frac{\partial v(x_{\tau})}{\partial x_{\tau}}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{W}_{t}}\frac{\partial\alpha_{t,j}(x)}{\partial x_{\tau}}\,v(x_{j}).

Taking operator norms and using
\|\partial v(x_{\tau})/\partial x_{\tau}\|\leq L_{v}
and
\|v(x_{j})\|\leq V_{R}
yields

\Big\|\frac{\partial y_{t}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|\leq\alpha_{t,\tau}L_{v}+V_{R}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{W}_{t}}\Big\|\frac{\partial\alpha_{t,j}}{\partial x_{\tau}}\Big\|.

Under the shared regime in Section
4.2.2
,
\alpha_{t,\tau}\leq c_{2}/|\mathcal{W}_{t}|
and

\sum_{j\in\mathcal{W}_{t}}\|\partial\alpha_{t,j}/\partial x_{\tau}\|\leq L_{\alpha}/|\mathcal{W}_{t}|
, hence

\|\partial y_{t}/\partial x_{\tau}\|\lesssim 1/|\mathcal{W}_{t}|
.
For full-prefix attention
|\mathcal{W}_{t}|=t+1
, recovering
\|\partial y_{t}/\partial x_{\tau}\|\lesssim 1/(t+1)
.

(2) Mamba under failed freeze time.

Item (2) follows by combining Proposition
4
with failed freeze time, namely

\sum_{r=\tau+1}^{t}\Delta_{r}(x)\geq c_{\Delta}(t-\tau),

that is, by Corollary
4.6
.
∎

Appendix D
BIBO stability on infinite horizons and uniform-in-
T
bounds

We extend the finite-horizon BIBO statement to infinite sequences under an explicit
row-contraction condition, and to uniform-in-
T
bounds for truncated length-
T
networks
without appealing to compactness.

D.1
Sequence norms and stability definition

We use the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,2}
and balls from Definition
7
.
For finite tensors we also use the comparison (
35
).

D.2
Feedback matrix and row-contraction condition

Fix a causal width-
m
Sessa block
G
as in Section
3.1
, but now acting on
infinite sequences in
\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}^{m})
.
We emphasize that the block input and output live in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, while the triangular solve

(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})s=f
is performed in a value space
\mathbb{R}^{r}
: in our definition,

s_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
f_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
g_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
, and
z_{t}=s_{t}\odot g_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
and the output projection is token-wise affine
o:\mathbb{R}^{r}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Causal feedback-attention weights.

For each input
x
, the masked softmax in the feedback branch defines strictly lower-triangular weights

(\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t\tau}(x))_{t,\tau\geq 0}
with

\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t\tau}(x)\geq 0,\qquad\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t\tau}(x)=0\ \text{for }\tau\geq t,\qquad\sum_{\tau<t}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t\tau}(x)=1\ \text{for }t\geq 1,

(45)

with the empty sum
=0
for
t=0
. These properties hold as follows: for
t\geq 1
each row
t
is a softmax over the finite set

\{0,\dots,t-1\}
, hence
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t\tau}\geq 0
and
\sum_{\tau<t}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t\tau}=1
; for
t=0
we set

\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{0\tau}=0
for all
\tau
, i.e. the context is empty, so the empty sum equals
0
.

Feedback attention matrix.

Define
\Alpha_{\mathrm{fb}}(x):=(\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t\tau}(x))_{t,\tau\geq 0}
.

Feedback coefficient and the Sessa matrix
B_{\mathrm{fb}}
.

By definition of the Sessa block, the feedback coefficient is

\gamma_{t}(x)=\tanh(u_{t}(x))\in(-1,1),

computed token-wise from the block input, via affine maps and element-wise nonlinearities.
Define the diagonal operator
\Gamma_{\mathrm{fb}}\!\left(x\right):=\mathrm{diag}(\gamma_{t}(x))_{t\geq 0}
and the
strictly lower-triangular matrix

B_{\mathrm{fb}}(x):=\Gamma_{\mathrm{fb}}\!\left(x\right)\,\Alpha_{\mathrm{fb}}(x)\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,\tau}(x)=\gamma_{t}(x)\,\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t\tau}(x).

(46)

Assumption 24

(Uniform feedback margin and row contraction)
.

For every radius
R\geq 0
there exists
\rho(R)\in[0,1)
such that for all inputs

x\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}^{m})
with
\|x\|_{\infty,2}\leq R
,

\sup_{t\geq 0}|\gamma_{t}(x)|\leq\rho(R).

(47)

In particular, using (
45
)–(
46
), for every
x
,

\sup_{t\geq 0}\sum_{\tau<t}|\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,\tau}(x)|=\sup_{t\geq 1}\sum_{\tau<t}|\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,\tau}(x)|=\sup_{t\geq 1}|\gamma_{t}(x)|\leq\sup_{t\geq 0}|\gamma_{t}(x)|\leq\rho(R)<1.

(
\star
)

Remark D.1

(An explicit choice of
ρ
(R))
.

If
u_{t}(x)
is produced by a token-wise feedforward stack of affine maps and element-wise
nonlinearities
\sigma
satisfying
|\sigma(z)|\leq|z|
coordinate-wise; this holds for
\mathrm{GELU}
.
Affine and linear maps are handled separately via spectral norms as in Lemma
D.2
. Then for some explicit constants
c_{\gamma}\geq 0
,
L_{\gamma,\mathrm{pre}}\geq 0

depending only on the block parameters,

\sup_{t\geq 0}|u_{t}(x)|\ \leq\ c_{\gamma}+L_{\gamma,\mathrm{pre}}\|x\|_{\infty,2}.

(48)

Hence on the ball
\|x\|_{\infty,2}\leq R
one can take

\rho(R):=\tanh(c_{\gamma}+L_{\gamma,\mathrm{pre}}R)\ <\ 1.

(49)

The strict inequality holds since
c_{\gamma}+L_{\gamma,\mathrm{pre}}R<\infty
and
\tanh(\cdot)<1
for finite
arguments.

D.3
Causal triangular solve on
\ell_{\infty}

The only operation that truly changes nature at
T=\infty
is the lower-triangular solve.
We treat it as a causal linear system.

D.4
Proof of Lemma
4.2

Proof.

Let
B_{\mathrm{fb}}=(\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,\tau})_{t,\tau\geq 0}
be strictly lower-triangular and define the causal operator

(B_{\mathrm{fb}}s)_{t}:=\sum_{\tau<t}\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,\tau}s_{\tau}
, a finite sum for each fixed
t
, acting on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
-valued sequences.
Here
\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,\tau}\in\mathbb{R}
is scalar and multiplies
s_{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
, i.e. scalar–vector multiplication.
Assume

\sup_{t\geq 0}\sum_{\tau<t}|\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,\tau}|\ \leq\ \rho\ <\ 1.

Then for every bounded input
f\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}^{r})
there exists a unique
bounded solution
s\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}^{r})
to

s=f+B_{\mathrm{fb}}s\qquad\text{equivalently, $(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})s=f$},

and it satisfies the explicit bound

\|s\|_{\infty,2}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{1-\rho}\,\|f\|_{\infty,2}.

(50)

Existence and uniqueness follow by forward substitution: for
t=0
,
s_{0}=f_{0}
; for
t\geq 1
,

s_{t}=f_{t}+\sum_{\tau<t}\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,\tau}s_{\tau}

depends only on previously defined
(s_{\tau})_{\tau<t}
. Thus a unique sequence
s
exists.

For the bound, define the partial maxima

M_{t}:=\max_{0\leq k\leq t}\|s_{k}\|_{2}\qquad(t\geq 0).

For
t=0
we have
s_{0}=f_{0}
, hence
M_{0}=\|s_{0}\|_{2}\leq\|f\|_{\infty,2}
.
For
t\geq 1
, using the row-sum estimate and
M_{t-1}\geq\|s_{\tau}\|_{2}
for all
\tau<t
,

\|s_{t}\|_{2}\leq\|f_{t}\|_{2}+\sum_{\tau<t}|\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,\tau}|\|s_{\tau}\|_{2}\leq\|f\|_{\infty,2}+\rho\,M_{t-1}.

We now prove by induction that for all
t\geq 0
,

M_{t}\leq\frac{1}{1-\rho}\,\|f\|_{\infty,2}.

The base case
t=0
holds since
M_{0}\leq\|f\|_{\infty,2}\leq\frac{1}{1-\rho}\|f\|_{\infty,2}
.
Assume the claim holds for
t-1
with some
t\geq 1
. Then the previous estimate gives

\|s_{t}\|_{2}\leq\|f\|_{\infty,2}+\rho\,M_{t-1}\leq\|f\|_{\infty,2}+\rho\,\frac{1}{1-\rho}\|f\|_{\infty,2}=\frac{1}{1-\rho}\|f\|_{\infty,2}.

Hence
M_{t}=\max\{M_{t-1},\|s_{t}\|_{2}\}\leq\frac{1}{1-\rho}\|f\|_{\infty,2}
, completing the induction.
Taking
\sup_{t\geq 0}
gives
\|s\|_{\infty,2}=\sup_{t}\|s_{t}\|_{2}=\sup_{t}M_{t}\leq\frac{1}{1-\rho}\|f\|_{\infty,2}
, which is (
50
).
∎

D.5
Explicit one-block bound without compactness

We now bound one Sessa block on
\ell_{\infty}
balls by tracking constants explicitly.

Lemma D.2

(Token-wise affine bound)
.

Let
y_{t}=x_{t}W+b
with
W\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d^{\prime}}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
, where the same
W
and
b
are used for all tokens.
Then for any sequence
x
, finite or infinite,

\|y\|_{\infty,2}\leq\|W\|_{2}\,\|x\|_{\infty,2}+\|b\|_{2},

where
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is the spectral norm for matrices and Euclidean norm for vectors.

Lemma D.3

(Causal attention is
\ell_{\infty}
-nonexpansive)
.

Let
\Alpha_{\mathrm{fb}}=(\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t\tau})
satisfy (
45
). Then for any value sequence
v
,
the sequence
y
defined by
y_{t}:=\sum_{\tau<t}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t\tau}v_{\tau}
satisfies

\|y\|_{\infty,2}\leq\|v\|_{\infty,2}.

Proof.

For
t\geq 1
,
y_{t}
is a convex combination of
\{v_{\tau}\}_{\tau<t}
, hence

\|y_{t}\|_{2}\leq\sup_{\tau<t}\|v_{\tau}\|_{2}\leq\|v\|_{\infty,2}.

For
t=0
the sum is empty, hence
y_{0}=0
and
\|y_{0}\|_{2}\leq\|v\|_{\infty,2}
as well.
Taking the supremum over
t\geq 0
gives
\|y\|_{\infty,2}\leq\|v\|_{\infty,2}
.
∎

Proposition 25

(One Sessa block: explicit ball-to-ball bound)
.

Consider one width-
m
Sessa block
G:\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}^{m})\to\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}^{m})
.
Assume:

•

the feedback matrix is
B_{\mathrm{fb}}(x)=\Gamma_{\mathrm{fb}}\!\left(x\right)\Alpha_{\mathrm{fb}}(x)
with
\Alpha_{\mathrm{fb}}(x)
satisfying (
45
) and

\gamma_{t}(x)=\tanh(u_{t}(x))
as above;

•

the block produces sequences
f(x),g(x)\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}^{r})
and an output projection

o:\mathbb{R}^{r}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
given token-wise by

o(z)_{t}=z_{t}W^{\mathrm{out}}+b^{\mathrm{out}},\qquad W^{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times m},\ \ b^{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m};

•

the block output is
G(x)=x+o(z)
with
z_{t}=s_{t}\odot g_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
and the solve is in value space:

z_{t}=s_{t}\odot g_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r},\qquad(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}}(x))s=f(x),\qquad s\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}^{r}).

Suppose there exist explicit constants
c_{f},c_{g},c_{\gamma}\geq 0
and
L_{f},L_{g},L_{\gamma,\mathrm{pre}}\geq 0
, depending only on the block parameters, such that for all inputs
x
,

\|f(x)\|_{\infty,2}\leq c_{f}+L_{f}\|x\|_{\infty,2},\quad\|g(x)\|_{\infty,2}\leq c_{g}+L_{g}\|x\|_{\infty,2},\quad\sup_{t}|u_{t}(x)|\leq c_{\gamma}+L_{\gamma,\mathrm{pre}}\|x\|_{\infty,2}.

(51)

Define, for
R\geq 0
,

\rho_{R}:=\tanh(c_{\gamma}+L_{\gamma,\mathrm{pre}}R)\in[0,1),\qquad F_{R}:=c_{f}+L_{f}R,\qquad G_{R}:=c_{g}+L_{g}R.

Then for all
x
with
\|x\|_{\infty,2}\leq R
, the block output satisfies the explicit bound

\|G(x)\|_{\infty,2}\;\leq\;R\;+\;\|W^{\mathrm{out}}\|_{2}\,\frac{F_{R}\,G_{R}}{1-\rho_{R}}\;+\;\|b^{\mathrm{out}}\|_{2}.

(52)

Proof.

On
\|x\|_{\infty,2}\leq R
, (
51
) gives
\|f\|_{\infty,2}\leq F_{R}
and

\|g\|_{\infty,2}\leq G_{R}
. Also
\sup_{t}|u_{t}(x)|\leq c_{\gamma}+L_{\gamma,\mathrm{pre}}R
, hence

\sup_{t}|\gamma_{t}(x)|\leq\rho_{R}
.
Using (

⋆

‣
24
), we get
\sup_{t}\sum_{\tau<t}|\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,\tau}(x)|\leq\rho_{R}<1
.
Lemma
4.2
then yields

\|s\|_{\infty,2}\leq\frac{1}{1-\rho_{R}}\|f\|_{\infty,2}\leq\frac{F_{R}}{1-\rho_{R}}.

For the element-wise product in
\mathbb{R}^{r}
, for each
t
,

\|z_{t}\|_{2}=\|s_{t}\odot g_{t}\|_{2}\leq\|s_{t}\|_{2}\,\|g_{t}\|_{2},

since

\|s_{t}\odot g_{t}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i}s_{ti}^{2}g_{ti}^{2}\leq\sum_{i}s_{ti}^{2}\Big(\sum_{j}g_{tj}^{2}\Big)=\|s_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\|g_{t}\|_{2}^{2}.

Hence

\|z\|_{\infty,2}\leq\|s\|_{\infty,2}\,\|g\|_{\infty,2}\leq\frac{F_{R}}{1-\rho_{R}}\,G_{R}.

Finally, by Lemma
D.2
for
o(z)=zW_{o}+b_{o}
and the residual
G(x)=x+o(z)
,

\|G(x)\|_{\infty,2}\leq\|x\|_{\infty,2}+\|o(z)\|_{\infty,2}\leq R+\|W^{\mathrm{out}}\|_{2}\|z\|_{\infty,2}+\|b^{\mathrm{out}}\|_{2},

which gives (
52
).
∎

Remark D.4

(Explicit dependence of the constants in (
51
))
.

Each branch, including the query, key, and value maps and the MLPs producing
f
,
g
, and
u
and related components,
is a finite composition of token-wise affine maps,
\mathrm{RoPE}_{t}
rotations that are orthogonal and norm-preserving,
masked softmax attention as in Lemma
D.3
, and element-wise nonlinearities whose growth is at most linear on bounded sets.
The solve
(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})s=f
and the Hadamard product
z=s\odot g
take place in the value space
\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
while the output projection
o:\mathbb{R}^{r}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is token-wise affine.
Thus one can always choose
c_{\bullet}
and
L_{\bullet}
explicitly from the operator norms of the weight matrices involved
and the norms of the biases, by repeated use of Lemma
D.2
and the inequality
\|\mathrm{GELU}(v)\|_{2}\leq\|v\|_{2}
.

Appendix E
Polynomial decay of token influence in the feedback recursion

E.1
Scalar recursion and impulse response

We work on discrete time
t\in\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\dots\}
.
Let
(\gamma_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a sequence in
\mathbb{R}
, and let

\{\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}\}_{t\geq 1,\ 0\leq j<t}
be nonnegative weights such that, for every
t\geq 1
,

\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}\geq 0,\qquad\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}\leq 1.

(53)

Given an input sequence
(f_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, consider the recursion

y_{0}=f_{0},\qquad y_{t}=f_{t}+\gamma_{t}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}y_{j},\quad t\geq 1.

(54)

To isolate the influence of a single token, we consider the impulse input at time
0
:

f_{0}=1,\qquad f_{t}=0\ \text{for }t\geq 1,

so that (
54
) reduces to the impulse response recursion

\begin{cases}y_{0}=1,\\[5.69054pt]
y_{t}=\gamma_{t}\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}y_{j},\quad t\geq 1.\end{cases}

(55)

In the full vector model,
y_{t}
can be interpreted as a scalar influence coefficient, e.g. an entry of
(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})^{-1}
.

E.2
Assumptions

Assumption 26

(Upper envelope on attention)
.

There exists a constant
c_{2}\in(0,\infty)
such that for all
t\geq 1
and all
0\leq j<t
,

\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}\leq\frac{c_{2}}{t},\qquad\text{and \eqref{eq:alpha_simplex} holds.}

(56)

Remark E.1

(On the size of
c_{2}
)
.

Under (
53
) with
\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}\leq 1
, the conclusion
c_{2}\geq 1
no longer follows.
If one additionally has
\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}=1
for all
t
, then
c_{2}\geq 1
is necessary.

Assumption 27

(Bounded feedback)
.

There exists
\gamma_{\max}\in[0,1)
such that for all
t\geq 0
,

|\gamma_{t}|\leq\gamma_{\max}.

(57)

Define the feedback mass parameter

\eta:=\gamma_{\max}c_{2},

(58)

and assume the nontrivial feedback regime

0<\eta<1.

(59)

Equivalently, define the tail exponent

\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}:=1-\eta=1-\gamma_{\max}c_{2}\in(0,1],

(60)

so that
\eta=1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}
.

Remark E.2

(Degenerate case
\eta=0
)
.

If
\eta=0
then
\gamma_{\max}=0
and hence
\gamma_{t}=0
for all
t
.
The recursion (
55
) has no feedback and the impulse response is trivial:

y_{0}=1
and
y_{t}=0
for all
t\geq 1
.
We therefore focus on
0<\eta<1
when stating a genuine power-law tail.

E.3
Bounded logits imply near-uniform softmax weights

Bounded logits imply near-uniform softmax weights.

This is an immediate specialization of Lemma
C.1
.
Indeed, fix
t\geq 1
and take the index set
\mathcal{I}=\{0,\dots,t-1\}
with
n=|\mathcal{I}|=t
.
If the logits satisfy
\beth_{\min}\leq\beth_{t,j}\leq\beth_{\max}
for all
j\in\mathcal{I}
, then the spread is

\Delta_{0}=\beth_{\max}-\beth_{\min}
, and Lemma
C.1
gives, for all
j<t
,

\frac{e^{\beth_{\min}-\beth_{\max}}}{t}\ \leq\ \alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}\ \leq\ \frac{e^{\beth_{\max}-\beth_{\min}}}{t}.

(61)

In particular, Assumption
26
holds with
c_{2}=e^{\beth_{\max}-\beth_{\min}}
.

E.4
Polynomial decay theorem

Theorem 28

(Polynomial decay of the impulse response)
.

Consider the impulse recursion (
55
). Suppose Assumptions
26

and
27
hold and
0<\eta=\gamma_{\max}c_{2}<1
; equivalently
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}=1-\eta\in(0,1)
.
Then for all
t\geq 1
,

|y_{t}|\leq C\,t^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}},\qquad\text{where one may take}\qquad C:=(1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}})\,e^{\,1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}=\eta e^{\eta}.

(62)

In particular, since
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}>0
, we have
\lim_{t\to\infty}y_{t}=0
.

Proof.

Assume
0<\eta<1
. The degenerate case
\eta=0
is covered by Remark
E.2
.
Let
z_{t}:=|y_{t}|
. From (
55
) and Assumptions
26
–
27
,
for
t\geq 1
,

z_{t}=\Bigl|\gamma_{t}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}y_{j}\Bigr|\leq|\gamma_{t}|\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}|y_{j}|\leq\gamma_{\max}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}z_{j}.

Define the comparison sequence
(\tilde{y}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by

\tilde{y}_{0}=1,\qquad\tilde{y}_{t}=\gamma_{\max}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}\tilde{y}_{j},\quad t\geq 1.

(63)

By induction on
t
, using
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}\geq 0
, we have
z_{t}\leq\tilde{y}_{t}
for all
t
, hence

|y_{t}|=z_{t}\leq\tilde{y}_{t}\qquad\forall t.

(64)

Let
s_{t}:=\sum_{k=0}^{t}\tilde{y}_{k}
. Since
\tilde{y}_{k}\geq 0
, the sequence
s_{t}
is increasing and
s_{t}\geq 1
.
Using (
63
) and
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}\leq c_{2}/t
we obtain, for
t\geq 1
,

\tilde{y}_{t}\;=\gamma_{\max}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}\tilde{y}_{j}\leq\gamma_{\max}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\frac{c_{2}}{t}\tilde{y}_{j}=\frac{\eta}{t}\,s_{t-1}.

Therefore,

s_{t}=s_{t-1}+\tilde{y}_{t}\leq s_{t-1}+\frac{\eta}{t}s_{t-1}=s_{t-1}\Bigl(1+\frac{\eta}{t}\Bigr),\qquad t\geq 1.

(65)

Taking logarithms and using
\log(1+x)\leq x
for
x>-1
,

\log s_{n}\leq\log s_{0}+\sum_{t=1}^{n}\log\Bigl(1+\frac{\eta}{t}\Bigr)\leq\sum_{t=1}^{n}\frac{\eta}{t}=\eta H_{n},

where
H_{n}=\sum_{t=1}^{n}\frac{1}{t}
is the
n
-th harmonic number.
Using
H_{n}\leq 1+\log n
for
n\geq 1
gives

s_{n}\leq e^{\eta}n^{\eta}\qquad\forall n\geq 1.

(66)

Finally, for
t\geq 1
we use
s_{t-1}\leq s_{t}
and (
66
):

\tilde{y}_{t}\leq\frac{\eta}{t}s_{t-1}\leq\frac{\eta}{t}s_{t}\leq\frac{\eta}{t}\,e^{\eta}t^{\eta}=\eta e^{\eta}\,t^{\eta-1}.

Since
\eta-1=-(1-\eta)=-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}
, we obtain
\tilde{y}_{t}\leq\eta e^{\eta}\,t^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}
.
Combining with (
64
) yields (
62
) with
C=\eta e^{\eta}
.
∎

E.5
Finite-horizon formulation

Corollary E.3

(Finite-horizon bound)
.

Fix
T\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and consider (
55
) only for
t\in\{0,1,\dots,T-1\}
.
Assume that Assumptions
26
and
27
hold for all
1\leq t\leq T-1

with the same constants
c_{2}
and
\gamma_{\max}
, and
0<\eta=\gamma_{\max}c_{2}<1
; equivalently
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}=1-\eta\in(0,1)
.
Then (
62
) holds for all
t\in\{1,\dots,T-1\}
with the same constant
C=\eta e^{\eta}=(1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}})e^{\,1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate restriction of Theorem
28
to
1\leq t\leq T-1
.
∎

E.6
Impulse at an arbitrary position
j

Corollary E.4

(Decay from an impulse at position
j
)
.

Fix an index
j\geq 0
. Consider (
54
) with the impulse input at
j
:

f_{j}=1,\qquad f_{t}=0\ \text{for }t\neq j,

and with
y_{t}=0
for
t<j
. Equivalently,
y_{0}=0
if
j>0
and the recursion is started from
t=j
.
Assume Assumptions
26
–
27
and
0<\eta=\gamma_{\max}c_{2}<1
; equivalently
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}=1-\eta\in(0,1)
.
Then for all
t>j
,

|y_{t}|\leq C\,(t-j)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}},\qquad\text{where one may take}\qquad C:=\eta e^{\eta}=(1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}})e^{\,1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

Proof.

Define
u_{n}:=|y_{j+n}|
for
n\geq 0
. Then
u_{0}=|y_{j}|=1
.
For
n\geq 1
, since
y_{k}=0
for
k<j
and
\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{j+n,k}\geq 0
,

u_{n}=|y_{j+n}|=\Bigl|\gamma_{j+n}\sum_{k=0}^{j+n-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{j+n,k}y_{k}\Bigr|\leq|\gamma_{j+n}|\sum_{k=j}^{j+n-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{j+n,k}|y_{k}|\leq\gamma_{\max}\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{j+n,j+r}\,u_{r}.

Moreover, by Assumption
26
,

\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{j+n,j+r}\leq\frac{c_{2}}{j+n}\leq\frac{c_{2}}{n}\qquad(n\geq 1),

since
j+n\geq n
.
Thus the sequence
u_{n}
satisfies the same comparison inequality as in the proof of
Theorem
28
, with the same
\gamma_{\max}
and the envelope
c_{2}/n
,
so repeating that argument yields

u_{n}\leq\eta e^{\eta}\,n^{\eta-1}=(\eta e^{\eta})\,n^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

Substituting
n=t-j
yields the claim.
∎

Appendix F
Tightness of the polynomial tail in a realizable regime

This section complements Theorem
28
with the upper bound
O(\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}})

by exhibiting a concrete diffuse routing regime in which the impulse influence is
exactly polynomial, that is,
\Theta(\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}})
.
This eliminates the semantic ambiguity that an upper bound alone does not preclude
faster decay, for instance exponential decay.

F.1
Gamma-ratio inequality of Gautschi

Lemma F.1

(Gautschi inequality for
0<\gamma<1
)
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
t\geq 1
be an integer. Then

(t+1)^{\gamma-1}\;\leq\;\frac{\Gamma(t+\gamma)}{\Gamma(t+1)}\;\leq\;t^{\gamma-1}.

(67)

Equivalently, with
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}:=1-\gamma\in(0,1)
,

(t+1)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\leq\frac{\Gamma(t+\gamma)}{\Gamma(t+1)}\leq t^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

Proof.

By Gautschi’s inequality
(
22
)
, for
x>0
and
0<\gamma<1
,

x^{1-\gamma}<\frac{\Gamma(x+1)}{\Gamma(x+\gamma)}<(x+1)^{1-\gamma}.

Setting
x=t
and taking reciprocals yields

(t+1)^{\gamma-1}\leq\frac{\Gamma(t+\gamma)}{\Gamma(t+1)}\leq t^{\gamma-1},

which is (
67
).
∎

F.2
Uniform routing yields a
\Theta(\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}})
tail

We consider the scalar impulse recursion from Section
E
:

y_{0}=f_{0},\qquad y_{t}=f_{t}+\gamma_{t}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}y_{j},\quad t\geq 1.

(68)

Proposition 29

(Tightness under uniform routing)
.

Assume uniform routing, which is maximally diffuse, and constant positive feedback:

\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}=\frac{1}{t}\mathbf{1}[j<t],\qquad\gamma_{t}\equiv\gamma\in(0,1).

Consider an impulse at time
0
:
f_{0}=1
and
f_{t}=0
for all
t\geq 1
.
Then for every
t\geq 1
the impulse influence admits the closed form

y_{t}=\frac{\gamma}{\Gamma(1+\gamma)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(t+\gamma)}{\Gamma(t+1)}.

(69)

Consequently, letting
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}:=1-\gamma\in(0,1)
, one has the two-sided bound

\frac{\gamma}{\Gamma(1+\gamma)}\,(t+1)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\;\leq\;y_{t}\;\leq\;\frac{\gamma}{\Gamma(1+\gamma)}\,t^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}},\qquad t\geq 1,

(70)

and in particular

y_{t}=\Theta(t^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}})\quad\text{and hence}\quad y_{t}=\Omega(t^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}).

Proof.

Define partial sums
S_{t}:=\sum_{k=0}^{t}y_{k}
. Under the stated assumptions and for
t\geq 1
,

y_{t}=\frac{\gamma}{t}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}y_{j}=\frac{\gamma}{t}S_{t-1},\qquad S_{t}=S_{t-1}+y_{t}=S_{t-1}\Big(1+\frac{\gamma}{t}\Big),

with
S_{0}=y_{0}=f_{0}=1
. Thus

S_{t}=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\Big(1+\frac{\gamma}{i}\Big)=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\frac{i+\gamma}{i}=\frac{\Gamma(t+1+\gamma)}{\Gamma(1+\gamma)\Gamma(t+1)}.

Using
y_{t}=\frac{\gamma}{t}S_{t-1}
and
\Gamma(t+1)=t\,\Gamma(t)
gives

y_{t}=\frac{\gamma}{t}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(t+\gamma)}{\Gamma(1+\gamma)\Gamma(t)}=\frac{\gamma}{\Gamma(1+\gamma)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(t+\gamma)}{\Gamma(t+1)},

which is (
69
). The two-sided bound (
70
) follows directly
from Lemma
F.1
with
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}=1-\gamma
.
∎

Corollary F.2

(Uniform routing with an impulse at an arbitrary source position)
.

Assume the same explicit uniform-routing regime as in Proposition
29
:

\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}=\frac{1}{t}\mathbf{1}[j<t],\qquad\gamma_{t}\equiv\gamma\in(0,1).

Consider an impulse at time
\tau\geq 0
, i.e.

f_{\tau}=1,\qquad f_{t}=0\ \text{for }t\neq\tau,

with
y_{t}=0
for
t<\tau
. Then for every
\ell\geq 1
,

y_{\tau+\ell}=\gamma\,\frac{\Gamma(\tau+1)}{\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(\tau+\ell+\gamma)}{\Gamma(\tau+\ell+1)}.

(71)

Consequently, with
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}:=1-\gamma\in(0,1)
, for every fixed source position
\tau
,

y_{\tau+\ell}=\Theta_{\tau}(\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}})\qquad(\ell\to\infty).

Moreover, the prefactor depends on
\tau
and satisfies

\gamma\,\frac{\Gamma(\tau+1)}{\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)}\asymp\tau^{-\gamma}\qquad(\tau\to\infty).

In particular, there is no positive lower constant
c_{-}>0
such that

y_{\tau+\ell}\geq c_{-}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}

for all source positions
\tau
and all
\ell\geq 1
on an unbounded horizon.

Proof.

Define partial sums

S_{t}:=\sum_{k=\tau}^{t}y_{k},\qquad t\geq\tau.

Then
S_{\tau}=y_{\tau}=1
. For
t\geq\tau+1
, the recursion gives

y_{t}=\frac{\gamma}{t}\sum_{j=\tau}^{t-1}y_{j}=\frac{\gamma}{t}S_{t-1},\qquad S_{t}=S_{t-1}+y_{t}=S_{t-1}\Bigl(1+\frac{\gamma}{t}\Bigr).

Hence, for every
t\geq\tau+1
,

S_{t}=\prod_{i=\tau+1}^{t}\Bigl(1+\frac{\gamma}{i}\Bigr)=\prod_{i=\tau+1}^{t}\frac{i+\gamma}{i}=\frac{\Gamma(t+1+\gamma)\Gamma(\tau+1)}{\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)\Gamma(t+1)}.

Using
y_{t}=\frac{\gamma}{t}S_{t-1}
and
\Gamma(t+1)=t\,\Gamma(t)
yields

y_{t}=\frac{\gamma}{t}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(t+\gamma)\Gamma(\tau+1)}{\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)\Gamma(t)}=\gamma\,\frac{\Gamma(\tau+1)}{\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(t+\gamma)}{\Gamma(t+1)}.

Setting
t=\tau+\ell
gives (
71
).

For fixed
\tau
, the factor

\gamma\,\frac{\Gamma(\tau+1)}{\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)}

is a positive constant depending only on
\tau
, while Lemma
F.1
gives

(\tau+\ell+1)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\leq\frac{\Gamma(\tau+\ell+\gamma)}{\Gamma(\tau+\ell+1)}\leq(\tau+\ell)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

Since
\tau
is fixed, this implies

\frac{\Gamma(\tau+\ell+\gamma)}{\Gamma(\tau+\ell+1)}=\Theta_{\tau}(\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}),

hence
y_{\tau+\ell}=\Theta_{\tau}(\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}})
.

The Gamma-ratio asymptotic gives

\frac{\Gamma(\tau+1)}{\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)}\asymp(\tau+1)^{-\gamma}\qquad(\tau\to\infty),

so the source-dependent prefactor decays polynomially with
\tau
.

Moreover, taking
\ell=1
in (
71
) gives

y_{\tau+1}=\gamma\,\frac{\Gamma(\tau+1)}{\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)}{\Gamma(\tau+2)}=\frac{\gamma}{\tau+1}.

Hence
y_{\tau+1}\to 0
as
\tau\to\infty
. Therefore no positive lower constant
independent of
\tau
can satisfy

y_{\tau+\ell}\geq c_{-}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}

for all source positions
\tau
and all
\ell\geq 1
on an unbounded horizon.
∎

Corollary F.3

(Uniform two-sided heavy-tail envelope on a bounded source family)
.

Fix
\tau_{\max}\in\mathbb{N}
. Under the regime of
Corollary
F.2
, there exist constants

c^{-}_{\tau_{\max},\gamma},c^{+}_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}>0
such that for every
source position
0\leq\tau\leq\tau_{\max}
and every
\ell\geq 1
,

c^{-}_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\leq y_{\tau+\ell}\leq c^{+}_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}},\qquad\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}:=1-\gamma.

In particular, the explicit uniform-routing regime realizes a uniform two-sided
heavy-tail envelope on every bounded source family, and hence on every fixed finite horizon.

Proof.

Write

a_{\tau}:=\gamma\,\frac{\Gamma(\tau+1)}{\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)}.

Since the set
\{0,\dots,\tau_{\max}\}
is finite and each
a_{\tau}
is positive,

m_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}:=\min_{0\leq\tau\leq\tau_{\max}}a_{\tau}>0,\qquad M_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}:=\max_{0\leq\tau\leq\tau_{\max}}a_{\tau}<\infty.

By Corollary
F.2
,

y_{\tau+\ell}=a_{\tau}\,\frac{\Gamma(\tau+\ell+\gamma)}{\Gamma(\tau+\ell+1)}.

Lemma
F.1
yields

(\tau+\ell+1)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\leq\frac{\Gamma(\tau+\ell+\gamma)}{\Gamma(\tau+\ell+1)}\leq(\tau+\ell)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

Therefore

y_{\tau+\ell}\leq M_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}\,(\tau+\ell)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\leq M_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

Also, since
0\leq\tau\leq\tau_{\max}
and
\ell\geq 1
,

\tau+\ell+1\leq\tau_{\max}+\ell+1\leq(\tau_{\max}+2)\ell,

hence

(\tau+\ell+1)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\geq(\tau_{\max}+2)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

Thus

y_{\tau+\ell}\geq m_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}\,(\tau+\ell+1)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\geq m_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}\,(\tau_{\max}+2)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\,\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}.

So one may take

c^{-}_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}:=m_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}\,(\tau_{\max}+2)^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}},\qquad c^{+}_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}:=M_{\tau_{\max},\gamma}.

∎

Consequence for the influence kernel

In the lower-triangular solve
s=Kf
with
K=(I-B_{\mathrm{fb}})^{-1}
, choosing

\big[B_{\mathrm{fb}}\big]_{t,j}=\gamma\,\alpha^{\mathrm{fb}}_{t,j}=\begin{cases}0,&t=0,\\[2.0pt]
\dfrac{\gamma}{t}\,\mathbf{1}[j<t],&t\geq 1,\end{cases}

yields that the column
K_{\cdot,0}
is precisely the impulse response
(y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
above.
Hence,

|K_{t,0}|=\Theta(t^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}),

so the polynomial envelope in Theorem
8
is sharp, and the rate is attained by a concrete heavy-tailed memory mode.

Remark F.4

(Impulse at time
\tau
)
.

Assume
\gamma\in(0,1)
.
The same computation applies to an impulse at time
\tau
. If
f_{\tau}=1
,
f_{t}=0
for
t\neq\tau
, and
y_{t}=0
for
t<\tau
, then for
t\geq\tau+1

y_{t}=\gamma\,\frac{\Gamma(t+\gamma)\Gamma(\tau+1)}{\Gamma(t+1)\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)}=C(\tau,\gamma)\cdot\frac{\Gamma(t+\gamma)}{\Gamma(t+1)},

with
C(\tau,\gamma):=\gamma\,\Gamma(\tau+1)/\Gamma(\tau+1+\gamma)>0
.
Hence, for
\ell=t-\tau
, the lag-
\ell
tail is again
\Theta(\ell^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}})
by Lemma
F.1
, in agreement with Corollary
E.4
.

Appendix G
Heavy-tail convolution estimates

Definition 9

(Discrete convolution on positive lags)
.

For nonnegative sequences
a,b:\mathbb{N}^{*}\to[0,\infty)
, define

(a*b)(n):=\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}a(n-m)b(m),\qquad n\geq 2,

and
(a*b)(1):=0
.
Inductively define
a^{(*1)}:=a
and
a^{(*k)}:=a^{(*(k-1))}*a
for
k\geq 2
.

Lemma G.1

(Discrete power convolution)
.

Let
\sigma,\rho>0
, and define

u_{\sigma}(n):=n^{\sigma-1},\qquad u_{\rho}(n):=n^{\rho-1},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}.

Then there exist constants
c_{\sigma,\rho},C_{\sigma,\rho}\in(0,\infty)
such that

c_{\sigma,\rho}\,n^{\sigma+\rho-1}\leq(u_{\sigma}*u_{\rho})(n)\leq C_{\sigma,\rho}\,n^{\sigma+\rho-1},\qquad n\geq 2.

Proof.

Fix
n\geq 2
.

For the upper bound, split the sum into the two regions

1\leq m\leq\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor+1\leq m\leq n-1.

If
1\leq m\leq n/2
, then
n-m\in[n/2,n-1]
, hence

(n-m)^{\sigma-1}\leq C_{\sigma}\,n^{\sigma-1},\qquad C_{\sigma}:=\max\{1,2^{1-\sigma}\}.

Therefore

\sum_{m=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(n-m)^{\sigma-1}m^{\rho-1}\leq C_{\sigma}n^{\sigma-1}\sum_{m=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}m^{\rho-1}.

Since
\rho>0
, the standard integral comparison gives

\sum_{m=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}m^{\rho-1}\leq 1+\int_{1}^{n/2}x^{\rho-1}\,dx\leq C^{\prime}_{\rho}\,n^{\rho}

for some constant
C^{\prime}_{\rho}
depending only on
\rho
. Hence

\sum_{m=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(n-m)^{\sigma-1}m^{\rho-1}\leq C_{\sigma}C^{\prime}_{\rho}\,n^{\sigma+\rho-1}.

If
\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1\leq m\leq n-1
, then
m\in[n/2,n-1]
, hence

m^{\rho-1}\leq C_{\rho}\,n^{\rho-1},\qquad C_{\rho}:=\max\{1,2^{1-\rho}\}.

Therefore

\sum_{m=\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}^{n-1}(n-m)^{\sigma-1}m^{\rho-1}\leq C_{\rho}n^{\rho-1}\sum_{m=\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}^{n-1}(n-m)^{\sigma-1}.

After the change of variable
r=n-m
, the inner sum becomes

\sum_{r=1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}r^{\sigma-1}\leq C^{\prime}_{\sigma}n^{\sigma}

for some constant
C^{\prime}_{\sigma}
depending only on
\sigma
. Hence

\sum_{m=\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}^{n-1}(n-m)^{\sigma-1}m^{\rho-1}\leq C_{\rho}C^{\prime}_{\sigma}\,n^{\sigma+\rho-1}.

Adding the two estimates proves the upper bound.

For the lower bound, restrict the sum to the central block

\left\lfloor\frac{n}{4}\right\rfloor\leq m\leq\left\lfloor\frac{3n}{4}\right\rfloor.

For every such
m
and every
n\geq 4
one has

\frac{n}{4}\leq m\leq\frac{3n}{4},\qquad\frac{n}{4}\leq n-m\leq\frac{3n}{4}.

Hence

m^{\rho-1}\geq c_{\rho}\,n^{\rho-1},\qquad(n-m)^{\sigma-1}\geq c_{\sigma}\,n^{\sigma-1},

where one may take

c_{\rho}:=\min\{1,4^{1-\rho}\},\qquad c_{\sigma}:=\min\{1,4^{1-\sigma}\}.

Indeed, if
\rho\leq 1
, then
m\leq n
implies
m^{\rho-1}\geq n^{\rho-1}
; if
\rho\geq 1
, then

m\geq n/4
implies
m^{\rho-1}\geq 4^{1-\rho}n^{\rho-1}
.
The same argument applies to
(n-m)^{\sigma-1}
.

Therefore every summand in the central block is bounded below by

c_{\sigma}c_{\rho}\,n^{\sigma+\rho-2}.

The number of integers in the central block is at least
n/2-2
. Consequently, for all
n\geq 8
,

(u_{\sigma}*u_{\rho})(n)\geq\left(\frac{n}{2}-2\right)c_{\sigma}c_{\rho}\,n^{\sigma+\rho-2}\geq\frac{c_{\sigma}c_{\rho}}{4}\,n^{\sigma+\rho-1}.

Since only finitely many values
2\leq n<8
remain, their minimum ratio to

n^{\sigma+\rho-1}
is positive. Adjusting the constant completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 30

(Heavy-tail convolution class)
.

Fix
\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}\in(0,1)
and define

f_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}(n):=n^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}.

Then, for every fixed
k\geq 1
, there exist constants

c_{k,\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}},C_{k,\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\in(0,\infty)
such that

c_{k,\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\,n^{k(1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}})-1}\leq f_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}^{(*k)}(n)\leq C_{k,\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}\,n^{k(1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}})-1},\qquad n\geq k.

(72)

Proof.

Set

\sigma:=1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}\in(0,1).

Then

f_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}(n)=n^{-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}=n^{\sigma-1}=u_{\sigma}(n).

We prove by induction on
k
that there exist constants

a_{k},b_{k}>0
such that

a_{k}\,n^{k\sigma-1}\leq u_{\sigma}^{(*k)}(n)\leq b_{k}\,n^{k\sigma-1},\qquad n\geq k.

(73)

For
k=1
, this is exactly

u_{\sigma}(n)=n^{\sigma-1}.

Assume now that (
73
) holds for some
k\geq 1
.

Fix
n\geq k+1
. By definition,

u_{\sigma}^{(*(k+1))}(n)=\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}u_{\sigma}^{(*k)}(n-m)u_{\sigma}(m).

For the upper bound, note that
u_{\sigma}^{(*k)}(r)=0
for
r<k
, since it is a

k
-fold convolution of positive-lag sequences. Hence, after enlarging
b_{k}

if necessary, we may write

u_{\sigma}^{(*k)}(r)\leq b_{k}\,r^{k\sigma-1}\qquad\text{for every }r\geq 1.

Therefore

u_{\sigma}^{(*(k+1))}(n)\leq b_{k}\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}(n-m)^{k\sigma-1}m^{\sigma-1}.

Applying Lemma
G.1
with exponents

k\sigma
and
\sigma
yields

u_{\sigma}^{(*(k+1))}(n)\leq b_{k+1}\,n^{(k+1)\sigma-1}

for some constant
b_{k+1}>0
.

For the lower bound, rewrite the sum using
r:=n-m
:

u_{\sigma}^{(*(k+1))}(n)=\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}u_{\sigma}^{(*k)}(r)\,u_{\sigma}(n-r).

Since
u_{\sigma}^{(*k)}(r)=0
for
r<k
, this becomes

u_{\sigma}^{(*(k+1))}(n)=\sum_{r=k}^{n-1}u_{\sigma}^{(*k)}(r)\,(n-r)^{\sigma-1}.

Applying the lower induction hypothesis on the range
r\geq k
gives

u_{\sigma}^{(*(k+1))}(n)\geq a_{k}\sum_{r=k}^{n-1}r^{k\sigma-1}(n-r)^{\sigma-1}.

Now write

\sum_{r=k}^{n-1}r^{k\sigma-1}(n-r)^{\sigma-1}=\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}r^{k\sigma-1}(n-r)^{\sigma-1}-\sum_{r=1}^{k-1}r^{k\sigma-1}(n-r)^{\sigma-1}.

By Lemma
G.1
, the full sum is bounded below by

c\,n^{(k+1)\sigma-1}

for some constant
c>0
depending only on
k
and
\sigma
.

On the other hand, since
k-1
is fixed,

\sum_{r=1}^{k-1}r^{k\sigma-1}(n-r)^{\sigma-1}\leq C\,n^{\sigma-1}

for some constant
C>0
depending only on
k
and
\sigma
.
Because
k\sigma>0
, one has

n^{\sigma-1}=o\!\left(n^{(k+1)\sigma-1}\right)\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Hence there exist constants
c^{\prime}>0
and
N_{k}
such that, for all
n\geq N_{k}
,

\sum_{r=k}^{n-1}r^{k\sigma-1}(n-r)^{\sigma-1}\geq c^{\prime}\,n^{(k+1)\sigma-1}.

Therefore, for all
n\geq N_{k}
,

u_{\sigma}^{(*(k+1))}(n)\geq a_{k}c^{\prime}\,n^{(k+1)\sigma-1}.

It remains to treat the finitely many values
k+1\leq n<N_{k}
.
For each such
n
, one has
u_{\sigma}^{(*(k+1))}(n)>0
because
n
can be written
as a sum of
k+1
positive integers. Hence the ratio

\frac{u_{\sigma}^{(*(k+1))}(n)}{n^{(k+1)\sigma-1}}

is positive for each of those finitely many
n
. Taking the minimum of these
finitely many positive ratios and
a_{k}c^{\prime}
gives a constant
a_{k+1}>0
such that

u_{\sigma}^{(*(k+1))}(n)\geq a_{k+1}\,n^{(k+1)\sigma-1}\qquad\text{for all }n\geq k+1.

This closes the induction.

Since
f_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}=u_{\sigma}
with
\sigma=1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}
, we obtain

f_{\beta_{\mathrm{tail}}}^{(*k)}(n)\asymp n^{k(1-\beta_{\mathrm{tail}})-1},\qquad n\geq k.

This is (
72
)
∎

Appendix H
Deep Jacobian estimates

H.1
Setup

Fix a depth
N_{\mathrm{layer}}\geq 1
, a finite horizon
T
, and a compact input set
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.
Let

h^{(0)}=x\in\mathcal{X}_{0},\qquad h^{(n_{\mathrm{layer}})}=F_{n_{\mathrm{layer}}}\bigl(h^{(n_{\mathrm{layer}}-1)}\bigr),\qquad n_{\mathrm{layer}}=1,\dots,N_{\mathrm{layer}},

where each
F_{n_{\mathrm{layer}}}
is causal and continuously differentiable on the relevant compact set

\mathcal{X}_{n_{\mathrm{layer}}-1}:=F_{n_{\mathrm{layer}}-1}\circ\cdots\circ F_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{0}).

For each layer
n_{\mathrm{layer}}
and each
0\leq\tau\leq t\leq T-1
, define the one-block Jacobian block

J^{(n_{\mathrm{layer}})}_{t,\tau}(u):=\frac{\partial F_{n_{\mathrm{layer}},t}(u)}{\partial u_{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D},\qquad u\in\mathcal{X}_{n_{\mathrm{layer}}-1}.

Define also the full end-to-end Jacobian blocks

J^{\mathrm{e2e},(N_{\mathrm{layer}})}_{t,\tau}(x):=\frac{\partial h_{t}^{(N_{\mathrm{layer}})}(x)}{\partial h_{\tau}^{(0)}(x)}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}.

For scalar lower-triangular kernels
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
on

\{(t,\tau):0\leq\tau\leq t\leq T-1\},

we use the standard kernel product

(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B})(t,\tau):=\sum_{j=\tau}^{t}\mathcal{A}(t,j)\mathcal{B}(j,\tau).

H.2
